Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\n(this is the start of the consultation)\n\n[Last Question]\nHi, welcome in—I'm Dr. [Name]. What brings you in to see me today?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 26 / no_occupation / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Distractibility\nmanifestation: \"The main thing is I get pulled off track by every little sound or thought, like I’ll be trying to work on an application and then a car outside, my phone, or some random idea sends me somewhere else before I even notice.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hi, Dr. [Name]. Yeah, um, thanks for seeing me. I guess I'm here because... well, things just aren't really working out for me right now. I've been trying to get a job, right? Because I don't have one right now, and it's been really frustrating just trying to get through the day-to-day stuff. 

The main thing is, I just... I get pulled off track by literally every little sound or thought. It's driving me crazy. Like, I’ll sit down at my desk, tell myself I'm going to focus, and I'll be trying to work on an application—which is already stressful enough—and then, I don't know, a car will drive by outside. And it's not even a loud car, but suddenly I'm looking out the window. Or my phone screen will light up, or honestly, even if the room is completely quiet, I'll just get some random idea in my head. Like I'll suddenly remember a movie I watched three years ago or wonder how a certain type of engine works, and that just... it sends me somewhere else entirely. I'll end up opening a new tab or walking into the kitchen, and it happens before I even notice I stopped doing what I was actually supposed to be doing. It's just exhausting, honestly, because I sit there for hours trying to do this one simple application and I just keep getting hijacked by my own surroundings.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, man, no, it's definitely not a new thing. It's... it's been like this for literally as long as I can remember. Like, if you go all the way back to elementary school, it was the exact same story, just, you know, a different setting. I remember my teachers—like, I had this one teacher in third grade, Mrs. Davis, and she was always, and I mean literally *always*, moving my seat. It was like I was on a world tour of the classroom. First I'd be by the window, then I'd be in the back row, then I'm sitting right next to her desk up front. 

And the reason she kept moving me was because I just couldn't stop talking out of turn. Like, a thought would pop into my head about, I don't know, a cartoon I saw or something my brother did, and I’d just blurt it out while she was in the middle of a math lesson. I just couldn't hold it in. And then there was the homework situation, which was a total disaster. I was constantly forgetting my homework. Half the time I wouldn't even write down what the assignment was, or if I actually managed to do it at home, I’d leave it on the kitchen table, or it would just vanish into the black hole at the bottom of my backpack. It drove my parents completely insane. 

And when we actually had to sit quietly and do work in class? Like those big packets of worksheets they’d hand out? I would just absolutely rush through them. I just wanted to be done so I could do something else, anything else, really, so I’d blow through the whole worksheet in five minutes and just make the most ridiculous, careless mistakes. Like, I’d add instead of subtract, or I’d skip every third question because my eyes just kind of jumped over it. They were always pulling me aside, telling me I needed to "slow down and check my work," but I just couldn't bring myself to do it once I thought I was finished. So, yeah, this whole not-being-able-to-focus thing? It's been happening since I was like seven years old, it just looks a little different now that I'm trying to be an adult and get a job.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Physical restlessness? Um, well... I mean, obviously kids are supposed to sit still in class, right? That's the whole point of school, you go in, you sit at the little desk with the cubby underneath, and you listen to the teacher. And I knew that. I really did. I knew the rules. But as far as actually feeling like I *had* to get up and move around... I don't really think so? 

I mean, I'm trying to picture myself back in Mrs. Davis's class—since I was just talking about her—and I remember being moved around a lot because of the talking, like I said, just blurting things out. And I remember the careless mistakes on the worksheets. But I don't have this vivid memory of, like, jumping out of my seat and running around the room when I wasn't supposed to. Or like, feeling this intense physical urge where my legs were vibrating and I just *had* to stand up. I think I mostly stayed in my chair? 

I mean, maybe I shifted around a bit, like anyone does when they're sitting in those hard plastic chairs for six hours a day. You know the ones, with the metal legs that always squeaked on the linoleum floor? But I wouldn't say I was constantly out of my seat or pacing around or anything like that. It was more... my mind was running a marathon, not my body, if that makes sense. Like, the restlessness was all up in my head, ping-ponging from one thought to the next, rather than me physically needing to do jumping jacks in the middle of a spelling test. So, yeah, I don't think I was the kid who couldn't physically stay in his seat. The talking, yes, absolutely, but the getting up and moving around... no, I don't really think that was my main issue back then.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, Dr. [Name]. Yeah, um, thanks for seeing me. I guess I'm here because... well, things just aren't really working out for me right now. I've been trying to get a job, right? Because I don't have one right now, and it's been really frustrating just trying to get through the day-to-day stuff. \n\nThe main thing is, I just... I get pulled off track by literally every little sound or thought. It's driving me crazy. Like, I’ll sit down at my desk, tell myself I'm going to focus, and I'll be trying to work on an application—which is already stressful enough—and then, I don't know, a car will drive by outside. And it's not even a loud car, but suddenly I'm looking out the window. Or my phone screen will light up, or honestly, even if the room is completely quiet, I'll just get some random idea in my head. Like I'll suddenly remember a movie I watched three years ago or wonder how a certain type of engine works, and that just... it sends me somewhere else entirely. I'll end up opening a new tab or walking into the kitchen, and it happens before I even notice I stopped doing what I was actually supposed to be doing. It's just exhausting, honestly, because I sit there for hours trying to do this one simple application and I just keep getting hijacked by my own surroundings.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me whether you've experienced this kind of distractibility and trouble focusing for as long as you can remember—like back in school as a kid—or is this something that's really only started recently?\n\nPatient: Oh, man, no, it's definitely not a new thing. It's... it's been like this for literally as long as I can remember. Like, if you go all the way back to elementary school, it was the exact same story, just, you know, a different setting. I remember my teachers—like, I had this one teacher in third grade, Mrs. Davis, and she was always, and I mean literally *always*, moving my seat. It was like I was on a world tour of the classroom. First I'd be by the window, then I'd be in the back row, then I'm sitting right next to her desk up front. \n\nAnd the reason she kept moving me was because I just couldn't stop talking out of turn. Like, a thought would pop into my head about, I don't know, a cartoon I saw or something my brother did, and I’d just blurt it out while she was in the middle of a math lesson. I just couldn't hold it in. And then there was the homework situation, which was a total disaster. I was constantly forgetting my homework. Half the time I wouldn't even write down what the assignment was, or if I actually managed to do it at home, I’d leave it on the kitchen table, or it would just vanish into the black hole at the bottom of my backpack. It drove my parents completely insane. \n\nAnd when we actually had to sit quietly and do work in class? Like those big packets of worksheets they’d hand out? I would just absolutely rush through them. I just wanted to be done so I could do something else, anything else, really, so I’d blow through the whole worksheet in five minutes and just make the most ridiculous, careless mistakes. Like, I’d add instead of subtract, or I’d skip every third question because my eyes just kind of jumped over it. They were always pulling me aside, telling me I needed to \"slow down and check my work,\" but I just couldn't bring myself to do it once I thought I was finished. So, yeah, this whole not-being-able-to-focus thing? It's been happening since I was like seven years old, it just looks a little different now that I'm trying to be an adult and get a job.\n\nDoctor: Growing up, did you often feel physically restless—like needing to get up, fidget, or move around—even when you were supposed to stay seated, such as at your desk in class?\n\nPatient: Physical restlessness? Um, well... I mean, obviously kids are supposed to sit still in class, right? That's the whole point of school, you go in, you sit at the little desk with the cubby underneath, and you listen to the teacher. And I knew that. I really did. I knew the rules. But as far as actually feeling like I *had* to get up and move around... I don't really think so? \n\nI mean, I'm trying to picture myself back in Mrs. Davis's class—since I was just talking about her—and I remember being moved around a lot because of the talking, like I said, just blurting things out. And I remember the careless mistakes on the worksheets. But I don't have this vivid memory of, like, jumping out of my seat and running around the room when I wasn't supposed to. Or like, feeling this intense physical urge where my legs were vibrating and I just *had* to stand up. I think I mostly stayed in my chair? \n\nI mean, maybe I shifted around a bit, like anyone does when they're sitting in those hard plastic chairs for six hours a day. You know the ones, with the metal legs that always squeaked on the linoleum floor? But I wouldn't say I was constantly out of my seat or pacing around or anything like that. It was more... my mind was running a marathon, not my body, if that makes sense. Like, the restlessness was all up in my head, ping-ponging from one thought to the next, rather than me physically needing to do jumping jacks in the middle of a spelling test. So, yeah, I don't think I was the kid who couldn't physically stay in his seat. The talking, yes, absolutely, but the getting up and moving around... no, I don't really think that was my main issue back then.\n\n[Last Question]\nThese days, do you still notice yourself frequently interrupting others, blurting out responses before someone finishes speaking, or acting on impulse without thinking things through?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 26 / no_occupation / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Interrupting_Intruding\nmanifestation: \"I cut people off a lot, especially my mom or the counselor, because an answer pops into my head and I say it before they’re done talking.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, yeah, definitely. That... that hasn't gone away at all. If anything, it's almost worse now because people expect you to be, you know, a polite adult who knows how to have a normal back-and-forth conversation. But I cut people off a lot. Like, *a lot*. 

It happens the most with my mom, or, um, this job counselor I've been seeing to try and get some help with the whole unemployment thing. I'll be sitting there in the counselor's office—and he's this really nice guy, always trying to give me these long explanations about resume building or interview skills—and he'll be halfway through a sentence, and an answer or a thought just pops into my head. And it's like... it's like if I don't say it right that exact second, it's going to disappear forever, or my brain is just going to get stuck on it and I won't hear anything else he says anyway. So I just say it. I blurt it right out before he's even close to done talking. 

And it's the exact same thing with my mom. I'm living with her right now, and she'll be telling me about her day or asking me a question, and I can already tell where her sentence is going. My brain just kind of jumps ahead to the end of the track. So I just answer her before she finishes asking. And she gets *so* frustrated with me. She's always sighing and going, "Can you please just let me finish a single thought?" And I feel terrible, I really do, because it's not that I don't care what she's saying, or that I'm trying to be disrespectful or rude. I'm really not. It's just that the thought is right there, right at the front of my brain, and my mouth just starts moving before I even realize I'm interrupting. It's like the connection between my brain and my mouth doesn't have a pause button. I'll literally tell myself before a conversation, "Okay, just listen, don't talk over them," but the second an idea hits me, it just slips out anyway.

for older adolescents and adults, returning calls, paying bills, keeping appointments).\n  manifestation: I keep forgetting regular stuff like texting people back, showing up on time for appointments, or bringing the papers my vocational counselor told me to bring.\n- key: S010\n  name: Fidgeting\n  description: Often fidgets with or taps hands or feet or squirms in seat.\n  manifestation: When I’m sitting with someone, my leg is usually bouncing, I’m clicking a pen, or I’m messing with my keys without even realizing I’m doing it.\n- key: S012\n  name: Feeling_Restless\n  description: Often runs about or climbs in situations where it is inappropriate. and I end up pacing from room to room.\n- key: S013\n  name: Quiet_Activity_Difficulty\n  description: Often unable to play or engage in leisure activities quietly.\n  manifestation: Quiet stuff is hard for me, because if my family is watching a movie or I try to sit and read, I start talking, getting up, or making noise without meaning to bother people.\n- key: S014\n  name: Constant_Activity\n  description: Is often 'on the go', acting as if 'driven by a motor'.\n  manifestation: Even on days when I don’t have work or training, I’m moving from the kitchen to the garage to my game setup like I’m supposed to be doing something all the time.\n- key: S017\n  name: Waiting_Turn_Difficulty\n  description: Often has difficulty waiting his or her turn.\n  manifestation: Waiting at the clinic or in line at the store gets me irritated fast, and I keep shifting around, checking the time, and wanting to just get it over with.\n- key: S018\n  name: Interrupting_Intruding\n  description: Often interrupts or intrudes on others (e.g., butts into conversations, games, or activities).\n  manifestation: I cut people off a lot, especially my mom or the counselor, because an answer pops into my head and I say it before they’re done talking.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: Onset prior to age 12 years\n  manifestation: Even in elementary school, teachers were always moving my seat because I talked out of turn, forgot homework, and rushed through worksheets with careless mistakes.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I’ve missed training deadlines and had to redo applications enough that I’m still not moving forward toward becoming an automotive technician, even though I know I can handle the hands-on work.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 10 months\n  manifestation: It’s been this disruptive for about 1 year and 10 months, ever since my soccer injury knocked me out of my usual routine.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 26 / no_occupation / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S008\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Can you tell me whether you've experienced this kind of distractibility and trouble focusing for as long as you can remember—like back in school as a kid—or is this something that's really only started recently?\n\nPatient: Oh, man, no, it's definitely not a new thing. It's... it's been like this for literally as long as I can remember. Like, if you go all the way back to elementary school, it was the exact same story, just, you know, a different setting. I remember my teachers—like, I had this one teacher in third grade, Mrs. Davis, and she was always, and I mean literally *always*, moving my seat. It was like I was on a world tour of the classroom. First I'd be by the window, then I'd be in the back row, then I'm sitting right next to her desk up front. \n\nAnd the reason she kept moving me was because I just couldn't stop talking out of turn. Like, a thought would pop into my head about, I don't know, a cartoon I saw or something my brother did, and I’d just blurt it out while she was in the middle of a math lesson. I just couldn't hold it in. And then there was the homework situation, which was a total disaster. I was constantly forgetting my homework. Half the time I wouldn't even write down what the assignment was, or if I actually managed to do it at home, I’d leave it on the kitchen table, or it would just vanish into the black hole at the bottom of my backpack. It drove my parents completely insane. \n\nAnd when we actually had to sit quietly and do work in class? Like those big packets of worksheets they’d hand out? I would just absolutely rush through them. I just wanted to be done so I could do something else, anything else, really, so I’d blow through the whole worksheet in five minutes and just make the most ridiculous, careless mistakes. Like, I’d add instead of subtract, or I’d skip every third question because my eyes just kind of jumped over it. They were always pulling me aside, telling me I needed to \"slow down and check my work,\" but I just couldn't bring myself to do it once I thought I was finished. So, yeah, this whole not-being-able-to-focus thing? It's been happening since I was like seven years old, it just looks a little different now that I'm trying to be an adult and get a job.\n\nDoctor: Growing up, did you often feel physically restless—like needing to get up, fidget, or move around—even when you were supposed to stay seated, such as at your desk in class?\n\nPatient: Physical restlessness? Um, well... I mean, obviously kids are supposed to sit still in class, right? That's the whole point of school, you go in, you sit at the little desk with the cubby underneath, and you listen to the teacher. And I knew that. I really did. I knew the rules. But as far as actually feeling like I *had* to get up and move around... I don't really think so? \n\nI mean, I'm trying to picture myself back in Mrs. Davis's class—since I was just talking about her—and I remember being moved around a lot because of the talking, like I said, just blurting things out. And I remember the careless mistakes on the worksheets. But I don't have this vivid memory of, like, jumping out of my seat and running around the room when I wasn't supposed to. Or like, feeling this intense physical urge where my legs were vibrating and I just *had* to stand up. I think I mostly stayed in my chair? \n\nI mean, maybe I shifted around a bit, like anyone does when they're sitting in those hard plastic chairs for six hours a day. You know the ones, with the metal legs that always squeaked on the linoleum floor? But I wouldn't say I was constantly out of my seat or pacing around or anything like that. It was more... my mind was running a marathon, not my body, if that makes sense. Like, the restlessness was all up in my head, ping-ponging from one thought to the next, rather than me physically needing to do jumping jacks in the middle of a spelling test. So, yeah, I don't think I was the kid who couldn't physically stay in his seat. The talking, yes, absolutely, but the getting up and moving around... no, I don't really think that was my main issue back then.\n\nDoctor: These days, do you still notice yourself frequently interrupting others, blurting out responses before someone finishes speaking, or acting on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Oh, yeah, definitely. That... that hasn't gone away at all. If anything, it's almost worse now because people expect you to be, you know, a polite adult who knows how to have a normal back-and-forth conversation. But I cut people off a lot. Like, *a lot*. \n\nIt happens the most with my mom, or, um, this job counselor I've been seeing to try and get some help with the whole unemployment thing. I'll be sitting there in the counselor's office—and he's this really nice guy, always trying to give me these long explanations about resume building or interview skills—and he'll be halfway through a sentence, and an answer or a thought just pops into my head. And it's like... it's like if I don't say it right that exact second, it's going to disappear forever, or my brain is just going to get stuck on it and I won't hear anything else he says anyway. So I just say it. I blurt it right out before he's even close to done talking. \n\nAnd it's the exact same thing with my mom. I'm living with her right now, and she'll be telling me about her day or asking me a question, and I can already tell where her sentence is going. My brain just kind of jumps ahead to the end of the track. So I just answer her before she finishes asking. And she gets *so* frustrated with me. She's always sighing and going, \"Can you please just let me finish a single thought?\" And I feel terrible, I really do, because it's not that I don't care what she's saying, or that I'm trying to be disrespectful or rude. I'm really not. It's just that the thought is right there, right at the front of my brain, and my mouth just starts moving before I even realize I'm interrupting. It's like the connection between my brain and my mouth doesn't have a pause button. I'll literally tell myself before a conversation, \"Okay, just listen, don't talk over them,\" but the second an idea hits me, it just slips out anyway.\n\n[Last Question]\nWould you say these focus and interrupting issues are currently causing real problems for you in more than one area of your life right now—like both in your job search and in your relationships with family or friends?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

Oh, man, yeah. Like, 100 percent. It's messing up basically everything right now. I mean, I already told you about how it's driving my mom crazy, right? Because we're living under the same roof and I'm just constantly talking over her or zoning out when she's trying to explain something simple to me, and it just creates this huge, heavy tension in the house all the time. 

But the job stuff... man, that's where it's really, really hurting me. Like, I have this goal, right? I want to be an automotive technician. I love cars. I'm actually really good with them. If you put an engine in front of me or tell me to replace a set of brake pads, I can do it. I know I can handle the hands-on work, no problem. I like working with my hands, it keeps me engaged, you know? But getting there? Actually getting through the process to get hired? It's been a total nightmare because of my brain just... not cooperating. 

There are all these forms and training modules you have to do, and I just can't focus on them. I'll sit down to fill out an application, and I'll skip a whole page of questions, or I'll forget to attach the right document because I got distracted by a YouTube video about transmissions halfway through, and then it gets rejected and I have to completely redo the whole application. I've had to redo so many of them. And the training deadlines! There were these online safety and certification things I was supposed to finish by a certain date to even get considered for this one apprentice program, and I just completely blew past the deadline. I kept thinking, "Oh, I'll do it tomorrow," or I'd open the tab, stare at the text for two minutes, and my mind would just wander off to a million other things. 

Because I missed those training deadlines, and because I've had to redo all these stupid forms, I'm just... stuck. I'm still not moving forward toward becoming a technician at all. It's just so incredibly frustrating because, like I said, I *know* I can do the actual job. Once I'm in the garage, getting my hands dirty, I'll be totally fine. But this whole hoop-jumping part with the paperwork and the deadlines and having to sit still and focus on a computer screen... it's like a brick wall. So yeah, it's definitely causing real problems everywhere. I just feel like I'm spinning my wheels and getting absolutely nowhere.

I've had to redo so many of them. And the training deadlines! There were these online safety and certification things I was supposed to finish by a certain date to even get considered for this one apprentice program, and I just completely blew past the deadline. I kept thinking, \"Oh, I'll do it tomorrow,\" or I'd open the tab, stare at the text for two minutes, and my mind would just wander off to a million other things. \n\nBecause I missed those training deadlines, and because I've had to redo all these stupid forms, I'm just... stuck. I'm still not moving forward toward becoming a technician at all. It's just so incredibly frustrating because, like I said, I *know* I can do the actual job. Once I'm in the garage, getting my hands dirty, I'll be totally fine. But this whole hoop-jumping part with the paperwork and the deadlines and having to sit still and focus on a computer screen... it's like a brick wall. So yeah, it's definitely causing real problems everywhere. I just feel like I'm spinning my wheels and getting absolutely nowhere.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I just couldn't hold it in. And then there was the homework situation, which was a total disaster. I was constantly forgetting my homework. Like, I’d add instead of subtract, or I’d skip every third question because my eyes just kind of jumped over it. I'll literally tell myself before a conversation, \"Okay, just listen, don't talk over them,\" but the second an idea hits me, it just slips out anyway.\n\nDoctor: Would you say these focus and interrupting issues are currently causing real problems for you in more than one area of your life right now—like both in your job search and in your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Oh, man, yeah. Like, 100 percent. It's messing up basically everything right now. I mean, I already told you about how it's driving my mom crazy, right? Because we're living under the same roof and I'm just constantly talking over her or zoning out when she's trying to explain something simple to me, and it just creates this huge, heavy tension in the house all the time. \n\nBut the job stuff... man, that's where it's really, really hurting me. Like, I have this goal, right? I want to be an automotive technician. I love cars. I'm actually really good with them. If you put an engine in front of me or tell me to replace a set of brake pads, I can do it. I know I can handle the hands-on work, no problem. I like working with my hands, it keeps me engaged, you know? But getting there? Actually getting through the process to get hired? It's been a total nightmare because of my brain just... not cooperating. \n\nThere are all these forms and training modules you have to do, and I just can't focus on them. I'll sit down to fill out an application, and I'll skip a whole page of questions, or I'll forget to attach the right document because I got distracted by a YouTube video about transmissions halfway through, and then it gets rejected and I have to completely redo the whole application. I've had to redo so many of them. And the training deadlines! There were these online safety and certification things I was supposed to finish by a certain date to even get considered for this one apprentice program, and I just completely blew past the deadline. I kept thinking, \"Oh, I'll do it tomorrow,\" or I'd open the tab, stare at the text for two minutes, and my mind would just wander off to a million other things. \n\nBecause I missed those training deadlines, and because I've had to redo all these stupid forms, I'm just... stuck. I'm still not moving forward toward becoming a technician at all. It's just so incredibly frustrating because, like I said, I *know* I can do the actual job. Once I'm in the garage, getting my hands dirty, I'll be totally fine. But this whole hoop-jumping part with the paperwork and the deadlines and having to sit still and focus on a computer screen... it's like a brick wall. So yeah, it's definitely causing real problems everywhere. I just feel like I'm spinning my wheels and getting absolutely nowhere.\n\n[Last Question]\nAside from these focus and impulsivity issues, have you been experiencing any ongoing feelings of sadness, excessive worry, or have you been using alcohol or recreational drugs regularly?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 26 / no_occupation / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Sadness? Um... no, not really. I mean, don't get me wrong, I get incredibly frustrated. Like, really, really annoyed with myself when I mess up another application or when my mom gets mad at me for interrupting again. It sucks, and I definitely have days where I'm just kicking myself, thinking, "Why can't you just do this one simple thing?" But it's not like... it's not a deep, dark sadness, you know? I'm not sitting in my room crying or feeling totally hopeless about life in general. I still want to get out there and work on engines and do stuff. I still get excited about things. It's just the roadblocks that make me mad, not sad. 

And as for worrying... I don't think I worry excessively. Honestly, my mom probably worries enough for the both of us right now. I don't really sit around chewing my fingernails about what's going to happen tomorrow or stressing out over every little detail. If anything, my problem is kind of the exact opposite, right? Like, I just don't think about the future or the consequences until the deadline has already blown right past me and it's too late. So no, I wouldn't say I'm an anxious person. I think my brain is just way too busy jumping to the next shiny thing to sit and worry about one specific thing for very long.

And the alcohol and drug thing—definitely not. I mean, I'll have a beer every now and then if a buddy comes over or if I'm watching a game or something. Just, you know, normal stuff. But I'm not drinking every day or getting drunk, and I definitely don't mess around with any recreational drugs at all. Honestly, tools and car parts are expensive enough as it is, so any money I do scrape together goes straight into that, not drugs. Plus, you know, a lot of the garages and dealerships I want to work at eventually? They do mandatory drug testing for their technicians. They have to, for insurance reasons and safety and all that, because you're working under heavy machinery and lifts. So I've always just steered clear of all that stuff entirely because I don't want to blow my chances before I even get my foot in the door. So yeah, no sadness, no crazy worrying, and no drugs or drinking problems. Just the... just the focus stuff and the blurting things out, really.

Because we're living under the same roof and I'm just constantly talking over her or zoning out when she's trying to explain something simple to me, and it just creates this huge, heavy tension in the house all the time. \n\nBut the job stuff... man, that's where it's really, really hurting me. Like, I have this goal, right? I want to be an automotive technician. I love cars. I'm actually really good with them. If you put an engine in front of me or tell me to replace a set of brake pads, I can do it. I know I can handle the hands-on work, no problem. I like working with my hands, it keeps me engaged, you know? But getting there? Actually getting through the process to get hired? It's been a total nightmare because of my brain just... not cooperating. \n\nThere are all these forms and training modules you have to do, and I just can't focus on them. I'll sit down to fill out an application, and I'll skip a whole page of questions, or I'll forget to attach the right document because I got distracted by a YouTube video about transmissions halfway through, and then it gets rejected and I have to completely redo the whole application. I've had to redo so many of them. And the training deadlines! There were these online safety and certification things I was supposed to finish by a certain date to even get considered for this one apprentice program, and I just completely blew past the deadline. I kept thinking, \"Oh, I'll do it tomorrow,\" or I'd open the tab, stare at the text for two minutes, and my mind would just wander off to a million other things. \n\nBecause I missed those training deadlines, and because I've had to redo all these stupid forms, I'm just... stuck. I'm still not moving forward toward becoming a technician at all. It's just so incredibly frustrating because, like I said, I *know* I can do the actual job. Once I'm in the garage, getting my hands dirty, I'll be totally fine. But this whole hoop-jumping part with the paperwork and the deadlines and having to sit still and focus on a computer screen... it's like a brick wall. So yeah, it's definitely causing real problems everywhere. I just feel like I'm spinning my wheels and getting absolutely nowhere.\n\nDoctor: Aside from these focus and impulsivity issues, have you been experiencing any ongoing feelings of sadness, excessive worry, or have you been using alcohol or recreational drugs regularly?\n\nPatient: Sadness? Um... no, not really. I mean, don't get me wrong, I get incredibly frustrated. Like, really, really annoyed with myself when I mess up another application or when my mom gets mad at me for interrupting again. It sucks, and I definitely have days where I'm just kicking myself, thinking, \"Why can't you just do this one simple thing?\" But it's not like... it's not a deep, dark sadness, you know? I'm not sitting in my room crying or feeling totally hopeless about life in general. I still want to get out there and work on engines and do stuff. I still get excited about things. It's just the roadblocks that make me mad, not sad. \n\nAnd as for worrying... I don't think I worry excessively. Honestly, my mom probably worries enough for the both of us right now. I don't really sit around chewing my fingernails about what's going to happen tomorrow or stressing out over every little detail. If anything, my problem is kind of the exact opposite, right? Like, I just don't think about the future or the consequences until the deadline has already blown right past me and it's too late. So no, I wouldn't say I'm an anxious person. I think my brain is just way too busy jumping to the next shiny thing to sit and worry about one specific thing for very long.\n\nAnd the alcohol and drug thing—definitely not. I mean, I'll have a beer every now and then if a buddy comes over or if I'm watching a game or something. Just, you know, normal stuff. But I'm not drinking every day or getting drunk, and I definitely don't mess around with any recreational drugs at all. Honestly, tools and car parts are expensive enough as it is, so any money I do scrape together goes straight into that, not drugs. Plus, you know, a lot of the garages and dealerships I want to work at eventually? They do mandatory drug testing for their technicians. They have to, for insurance reasons and safety and all that, because you're working under heavy machinery and lifts. So I've always just steered clear of all that stuff entirely because I don't want to blow my chances before I even get my foot in the door. So yeah, no sadness, no crazy worrying, and no drugs or drinking problems. Just the... just the focus stuff and the blurting things out, really."
  }
]

Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood. Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others. Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nOh, man, no, it's definitely not a new thing. It's... it's been like this for literally as long as I can remember. Like, if you go all the way back to elementary school, it was the exact same story, just, you know, a different setting. I remember my teachers—like, I had this one teacher in third grade, Mrs. Davis, and she was always, and I mean literally *always*, moving my seat. It was like I was on a world tour of the classroom. First I'd be by the window, then I'd be in the back row, then I'm sitting right next to her desk up front. \n\nAnd the reason she kept moving me was because I just couldn't stop talking out of turn. Like, a thought would pop into my head about, I don't know, a cartoon I saw or something my brother did, and I’d just blurt it out while she was in the middle of a math lesson. I just couldn't hold it in. And then there was the homework situation, which was a total disaster. I was constantly forgetting my homework. Half the time I wouldn't even write down what the assignment was, or if I actually managed to do it at home, I’d leave it on the kitchen table, or it would just vanish into the black hole at the bottom of my backpack. It drove my parents completely insane. \n\nAnd when we actually had to sit quietly and do work in class? Like those big packets of worksheets they’d hand out? I would just absolutely rush through them. I just wanted to be done so I could do something else, anything else, really, so I’d blow through the whole worksheet in five minutes and just make the most ridiculous, careless mistakes. Like, I’d add instead of subtract, or I’d skip every third question because my eyes just kind of jumped over it. They were always pulling me aside, telling me I needed to \"slow down and check my work,\" but I just couldn't bring myself to do it once I thought I was finished. So, yeah, this whole not-being-able-to-focus thing? It's been happening since I was like seven years old, it just looks a little different now that I'm trying to be an adult and get a job.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"

I'm not sitting in my room crying or feeling totally hopeless about life in general. I still want to get out there and work on engines and do stuff. I still get excited about things. It's just the roadblocks that make me mad, not sad. \n\nAnd as for worrying... I don't think I worry excessively. Honestly, my mom probably worries enough for the both of us right now. I don't really sit around chewing my fingernails about what's going to happen tomorrow or stressing out over every little detail. If anything, my problem is kind of the exact opposite, right? Like, I just don't think about the future or the consequences until the deadline has already blown right past me and it's too late. So no, I wouldn't say I'm an anxious person. I think my brain is just way too busy jumping to the next shiny thing to sit and worry about one specific thing for very long.\n\nAnd the alcohol and drug thing—definitely not. I mean, I'll have a beer every now and then if a buddy comes over or if I'm watching a game or something. Just, you know, normal stuff. Honestly, tools and car parts are expensive enough as it is, so any money I do scrape together goes straight into that, not drugs. Plus, you know, a lot of the garages and dealerships I want to work at eventually? They do mandatory drug testing for their technicians. They have to, for insurance reasons and safety and all that, because you're working under heavy machinery and lifts. So I've always just steered clear of all that stuff entirely because I don't want to blow my chances before I even get my foot in the door.
